(157)

These are two
independent
constraints on gauge parameters because, in the unrestricted canonical variation of the action, they multiply
independent
equations of motion
39
39

39

In DeWitt’s compact notation, the anomaly structure for any field variation
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\operatorname{\delta}\hskip-1.0pt}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\xi}\phi^{i}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}r^{i}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\xi^{\alpha}
in the action is given by

\int dt\,A\defeq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\operatorname{\delta}\hskip-1.0pt}_{\xi}S[\phi^{i}]{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}S_{,{i}}r^{i}_{\,\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}
.
Thus, the anomaly form

S_{,{i}}r^{i}_{\,\alpha}
, which acts on transformation parameters
\xi^{\alpha}
, is proportional to the left-hand sides of the equations of motion,
S_{,{i}}
, which may have linear dependencies when a gauge symmetry is present in the theory. Nontrivial anomaly-free conditions on
\xi^{\alpha}
determine the independent directions in the space spanned by
r^{i}_{\,\alpha}
that are not tangent to the extremal surface defined by
S_{,{i}}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}0
(i.e., that are not gauge directions). The number of these directions is given by the rank of

S_{,{j}{i}}r^{i}_{\,\alpha}\big|_{S_{,{i}}=0}
.
Dually, this rank corresponds to the number of independent normal directions to the extremal surface (i.e., on-shell independent rows of
S_{,{j}{i}}
) that are transverse to the vectors
r^{i}_{\,\alpha}
. These independent normal directions correspond to the independent on-shell conditions multiplying
\xi^{\alpha}
in the anomaly.

.
The second term on the right-hand side of (
155
), proportional to
\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N}^{\!{\hskip 0.8pt}\scriptscriptstyle{\perp}\!}F^{-1}\hskip-1.0pt\widetilde{\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral G\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.5pt}_{{\hskip-1.3pt\scriptscriptstyle{-}}}
, vanishes under the consistency condition
\widetilde{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N}^{\!{\hskip 0.8pt}\scriptscriptstyle{\perp}\!}F^{-1}}{\,\eomeq\,}0
, (
141
).
The first anomalous term in (
155
) is proportional to the
\widetilde{\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral G\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.5pt}_{{\hskip-1.3pt\scriptscriptstyle{-}}}\big((\partial\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N})\,\Omega{\,-\,}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N}^{\!{\hskip 0.8pt}\scriptscriptstyle{\perp}\!}F^{-1}\Theta\hskip 0.5pt\pi\hskip 0.5pt\big)
, which vanish on shell due to
(\partial\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N}){\,\eomeq\,}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N}^{\!{\hskip 0.8pt}\scriptscriptstyle{\perp}\!}F^{-1}\widetilde{U}_{0}
, where
\widetilde{U}_{0}(\gamma,\pi)
appeared in (
30
). The latter equation, besides
\widetilde{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N}^{\!{\hskip 0.8pt}\scriptscriptstyle{\perp}\!}F^{-1}}{\,\eomeq\,}0
, relies on the other consistency condition
(\partial\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}{N}){\,\eomeq\,}\dot{\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}\,\widetilde{U}_{0}
, (
141
). Thus, on-shell vanishing factors in the integrands of (
155
) encode two independent consistency conditions, constraining the pair of the Lagrange multipliers associated with the second-class constraints on the non-GR branch of the theory.

The degeneracy of operator
\widetilde{\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral G\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.5pt}_{{\hskip-1.3pt\scriptscriptstyle{-}}}
in the first equation of (
157
) ensures the emergence of a homogeneous gauge parameter
\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)
, which parameterizes the residual gauge freedom
associated with homogeneous time reparametrization. In the second equation, the one-mode degeneracy of
\widetilde{\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral G\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.5pt}_{{\hskip-1.3pt\scriptscriptstyle{-}}}
guarantees the existence of a solution for the transformation parameter
(\partial{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta})
. At the same time, since only
(\partial{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta})
is constrained, the transverse part of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta}^{n}
, associated with the spatial volume-preserving diffeomorphisms, remains arbitrary. Finally, there are no constraints on the symmetry parameterized by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\chi^{m}
, which is internal to the auxiliary sector and does not affect the metric-sector fields.

Thus, in its alternative formulation, the GUMG model possesses a gauge symmetry parameterized by the canonical transformations (
149
,
151
,
153
), subject to restrictions:

\begin{array}[]{|ll}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta}^{\!{\hskip 0.8pt}\scriptscriptstyle{\perp}\!}=F\,\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}\,,&\text{$\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)$ --- arbitrary homogeneous: $\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)$;}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta}^{n}=\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}\,{U^{n}_{0}}+\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta_{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}(\hskip-1.0ptt\hskip-1.0pt)}}^{n},&\text{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta_{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}(\hskip-1.0ptt\hskip-1.0pt)}}^{n}$ --- arbitrary transverse: $\,(\partial\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta_{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}(\hskip-1.0ptt\hskip-1.0pt)}}){\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}0$\,;}\\
\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\chi^{n}\,,&\text{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\chi^{n}$ --- arbitrary (internal to the auxiliary sector),}\end{array}

(158)

where
{U^{n}_{0}}(\gamma,\pi)
is defined in (
2.2
).
As a check, the appearance of
\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}\,{U^{n}_{0}}
in the constraint on
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta}^{n}
confirms the conjectured form of the homogeneous first-class Hamiltonian constraint in (
148
).

The first two gauge parameters,
\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta_{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}(\hskip-1.0ptt\hskip-1.0pt)}}^{n}
, in (
158
) fully parameterize the gauge freedom of the metric fields, which turns out to be the residual gauge symmetry inherited from the parental theory — the Einstein general relativity. Corresponding gauge restriction conditions on the left side of (
158
) effectively restrict the parameters
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta}^{\!{\hskip 0.8pt}\scriptscriptstyle{\perp}\!}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\zeta}^{n}
of the parental gauge symmetry (
3.4
) --- spacetime diffeomorphisms in the canonical gauge basis
40
40

40

This is strictly correct for the gauge transformations of the Lagrangian metric fields. The modified transformation law for
\pi^{{m}{n}}
can be matched to a parental counterpart by additional trivial gauge transformations.

.
Hypothetical modifications of the transformations for the auxiliary sector fields
\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\varLambda_{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.15}\mathbf{0}}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\nu^{n}
obviously cannot eliminate the anomaly. Similarly, local-in-time modifications of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\operatorname{\delta}\hskip-1.0pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\tau^{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}
also can not compensate it. As previously observed in Section
3.5
for
\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}
-GUMG models, the anomalous contributions can only be formally compensated by metric-dependent, time-nonlocal transformations of this field (in fact, only of its spatially averaged mode
\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\tau}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}}
).
Thus, it was demonstrated that, within the parameterized framework, the residual gauge transformations in the metric sector on the non-GR branch are either canonical or unhealthy due to time nonlocality. Conversely, all canonically-allowed, time-local gauge transformations correspond to restricted gauge transformations of the parental theory. This supports the analogous conclusions drawn within the original formulation
[
14
]
.

The gauge structure analysis above was performed for the canonical action
S_{par^{\prime\prime}}
, (
130
).
This choice was made for convenience and to clarify the origin and meaning of the gauge restrictions, which are more transparent in canonical representations.
Of course, the same gauge-restriction procedure could be applied directly in the Lagrangian representation (
132
), yielding the same result provided that the field-dependent coefficients of the constraints are tied to the restriction surface,
\pi^{{m}{n}}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}{\sqrt{\gamma\hskip 0.5pt}\hskip 0.5pt\vphantom{|}}(K^{{m}{n}}{\,-\,}\gamma^{{m}{n}}K)
, (
131
). The latter affects only the function
U_{0}^{m}(\gamma,\pi)
, (
2.2
), or its divergence
\widetilde{U}_{0}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}(\partial U_{0})
, (
30
), which depend on the trace of the metric momenta,
\hskip 0.5pt\pi\hskip 0.5pt
.

This concludes the analysis of the gauge structure in the parameterized alternative representation of GUMG. The gauge symmetry was already spatially nonlocal in local
\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}
-GUMG models, where only the homogeneous part of time reparametrizations remained a symmetry, while spatial diffeomorphisms were restricted to their transverse components. In general models, explicit nonlocality in the action and equations of motion further severes the nonlocalities by feeling the spatial inhomogeneity of
W({\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{\!$\sqrt{\gamma\hskip 0.5pt}\hskip 0.5pt\vphantom{|}$}}})
. However, we showed that the overall gauge structure remains analogous to that of
\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}
-GUMG. This consistency supports the validity of our approach to handling such nonlocalities.

4.5
Note on the effect of nonlocality on quantum measure

To conclude, we briefly address the impact of
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
-type spatial nonlocalities on the quantum measure. These nonlocalities may enter through Faddeev-Popov determinants and Jacobians of the
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
-dependent field redefinitions beyond any canonical field set.
A correct path integral measure follows from the canonical Liouville measure. In the Lagrangian representation (
132
), the non-ghost field sector retains the standard ultralocal GR measure, including the DeWitt determinant factor generated by integrating out gravitational momenta
41
41

41

Additionally, an ultralocal Jacobian may appear when passing from ADM metric variables to the Lagrangian metric field representation. However, auxiliary sector fields remain in their canonical representation, keeping their measure trivial.
.
However, in the representation (
134
), where the effective cosmological constant field
\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\varLambda
is introduced, the measure acquires an nonultralocal factor
\mathop{\mathrm{Det}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
due to the nonlocal field redefinition (
133
).
According to Lemma
A.4
, the determinant of
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
as a spatial operator with the two-point kernel
{E}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t|{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}^{\prime})
, (
123
), is

{\mathop{\mathrm{Det}}}{{}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)\;=\;\overline{W}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)\,\overline{W^{-1}}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)\,\geq\,1\,.

(159)

The full spacetime determinant of the operator
with the kernel
{E}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t|{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}^{\prime})\delta(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t{-}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t^{\prime})

follows as
42
42

42

Regularizing
\delta(0)
as
(\int\!d\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t\,1)^{-1}
ensures that this determinant remains
\geq 1
, with equality holding only when
W
is spatially homogeneous. For example, for
W{\,\equiv\>}\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}const
or
\det\gamma_{{m}{n}}(t,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})=\overline{\det\gamma_{{m}{n}}}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)
.

{\mathop{\mathrm{Det}}}{{}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt\;=\;e^{\delta(0)\int\!d\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t\,\ln\big(\overline{W}\,\overline{W^{-1}}\big)}\,.

(160)

Since
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
is a purely spatial nonlocality of a non-differential type, it does not introduce derivative expansions. Furthermore, as
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
arises solely from the spatial inhomogeneity of
W({\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{\!$\sqrt{\gamma\hskip 0.5pt}\hskip 0.5pt\vphantom{|}$}}})
, only a limited set of structures is expected to appear in its determinant.
This suggests that determinant contributions from such nonlocal structures remain relatively simple and manageable, at least for those arising from field redefinitions.

Computing the Faddeev-Popov determinants may be a more involved problem due to the complexity of the gauge structure. However, a well-chosen gauge fermion could simplify or even eliminate
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
-dependent nonlocalities. In any case, this lies beyond the scope of this work.

5
Conclusions

In this paper, we derived and analyzed the alternative representation of generalized unimodular gravity (GUMG), constructed by generalizing the Henneaux–Teitelboim (HT) approach to unimodular gravity.
We addressed the problem of consistent parameterization and examined the key dynamical and gauge features, including the emergence of explicit spatial nonlocality and its implications for the equations of motion and the gauge structure. We also confirmed that the gauge symmetry derived from canonical analysis matches the local-in-time part of the residual general-relativistic diffeomorphisms implied by the configuration-space restriction.

The alternative formulation is physically equivalent to the original setup of
[
12
,
14
]
, inheriting its non-regular constraint structure with two dynamical branches, each featuring a different number of physical degrees of freedom. On the non-GR branch — characterized here by a nonzero cosmological-constant field — an additional degree of freedom emerges, behaving as a cosmological perfect fluid with an equation of state governed by the barotropic parameter
W({\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{\!$\sqrt{\gamma\hskip 0.5pt}\hskip 0.5pt\vphantom{|}$}}})
, in agreement with results from the original formulation.

A distinctive feature of the alternative representation is the presence of the HT-like auxiliary field entering the action through a total spacetime divergence,
\partial_{\mu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
. The latter dynamically selects a spacetime-constant observable
\mathrm{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.15}\mathbf{0}}}
— the only free component of the cosmological-constant field in
\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\varLambda_{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.15}\mathbf{0}}}
parameterization (
132
). Consequently, the on-shell behavior (
136
) of the effective cosmological constant
\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\varLambda
emerges as a spatially nonlocal function, sensitive to normalized spatial variations of
W({\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{\!$\sqrt{\gamma\hskip 0.5pt}\hskip 0.5pt\vphantom{|}$}}})
.
The constant
\mathrm{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.15}\mathbf{0}}}
parameterizes a direction along which the space of solutions extends relative to general relativity (GR).
In GR, physical trajectories are confined to the constraint surface
{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}0
,
{H}_{n}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}0
. In contrast, the GUMG constraint surface extends to:

{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}{-}F^{-1}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}^{\,-1\!}\mathrm{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.15}\mathbf{0}}}\,{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}{-}F^{-1}\frac{W^{-1}}{\,\overline{\!W^{-1}\!}\,\vphantom{I^{|^{I}}}}\,\mathrm{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.15}\mathbf{0}}}
,

{H}_{n}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}0
,
where the first equation refines the secondary constraint
\mathcal{T}_{,{m}}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}0
using dynamical input. This explicit encoding of such an invariant in the action is advantageous within the restricted theory framework
[
20
]
, which suggests an interrelation between the effective actions of the parental and restricted gauge theories.

Another notable feature of the alternative formulation is the manifest appearance of spatial nonlocality in the action (
132
,
134
). Though arising in the course of consistent parameterization, this nonlocality is not an artifact of the formalism or an ill-chosen extension of configuration space but a genuine dynamical feature of GUMG models with nonconstant
W
. It was implicitly present already in the original canonical formulation, where the spatial average
\overline{F{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}}
defines a gauge-invariant constant of motion (taking on-shell equal to
-\mathrm{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.15}\mathbf{0}}}
), while the inhomogeneous secondary constraint
\widetilde{WF{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}}
is also gauge-invariant and vanishes on shell. Hence, their linear combination
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8ptF{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}
is a functionally complete (albeit spatially nonlocal) gauge-invariant integral of motion — the property, which the alternative formulation just makes explicit.

The
\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}
-GUMG subfamily (
9
), where the alternative parameterized formulation remains local, provides a useful particular case for understanding the dynamics and gauge structure of general GUMG. Its equations of motion are local, while gauge transformations exhibit the simple spatial nonlocality: spatially homogeneous time reparameterizations and transverse spatial diffeomorphisms.
In the general case with spatially varying
W({\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{\!$\sqrt{\gamma\hskip 0.5pt}\hskip 0.5pt\vphantom{|}$}}})
, the additional
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
-type nonlocality does not qualitatively alter the theory’s dynamics or gauge structure, making
\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}
-GUMG a full-fledged yet simpler setting for studying the GUMG behavior.

The framework also includes unimodular gravity (UMG) as the exceptional case (
10
), reproducing the Henneaux–Teitelboim action with restored diffeomorphism invariance
[
8
]
.
Interestingly, the time reparameterization symmetry restores not due to local parameterization alone, but rather from the secondary constraint becoming first-class in UMG. This constraint generates inhomogeneous time reparameterizations, while the missing homogeneous mode is effectively recovered via the homogeneous part of parameterization (the inhomogeneous auxiliary fields can be decoupled).
By contrast, in GUMG, the secondary constraint
(WF{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!})_{,{m}}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}0
is second-class, and time reparameterization remains broken, regardless of the applicability of the local parameterization scheme. As seen in
\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}
-GUMG case, where the local parameterization is consistent, yet full diffeomorphism symmetry is not restored. The new constraint introduced by the parameterization does generate a homogeneous gauge symmetry in the metric sector governed by the parameter
\overline{\color[rgb]{0.1,0,0.2}\varepsilon}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}t)
, while its inhomogeneous counterpart with
{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}
is fixed to zero by the secondary constraint and remains second-class, along with the longitudinal part of spatial diffeomorphisms.

Our constructive derivation confirms the equivalence between the original and alternative formulations of generalized unimodular gravity, both of which admit generalizations to a broader class of restrictions. The analysis presented provides an overview of the dynamical features and gauge structure of the theory. Nevertheless, various issues are still are still open.
While we have justified the applicability of the parameterized formalism in the case of manifolds with noncompact spatial sections from general grounds and specific properties of the
\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral E\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt
-type nonlocalities, a complete treatment of the theory still requires further development, including a more precise specification of asymptotic behavior of the fields.
In the broader GUMG framework, it would be valuable to explore cosmological solutions beyond the inflationary scenarios studied in
[
15
]
, including static configurations and other nontrivial spacetime geometries.
Potential global limitations on the solution space which may be hidden in subtle properties of GUMG-defining restriction also warrant further investigation.
In the context of Batalin-Vilkovisky quantization, it is of interest to clarify the relationship between the HT-like consistent parameterization scheme and the conversion-based approach, which is nontrivial even in the
\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}
-GUMG case. The homogeneous constraint
\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\lambda}^{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}(\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}}{+}F_{\!{\scriptscriptstyle{(\!\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}\!)}}}{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}})
can be restored via the homogeneous parameterization by extending the phase space with
\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\pi}{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}
,
\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}
. Then, following the conversion method
[
26
]
, one could attempt to convert the system of constraints with the two average-free second-class constraints:
\partial^{n}\hskip-1.0pt{H}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}F_{\!{\scriptscriptstyle{(\!\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptw}\!)}}}{H}_{{}^{{}_{\!\perp}}\!}}
, into a first-class set by introducing average-free auxiliary canonical pair:
\widetilde{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}
and
\widetilde{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.25}\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{0}}
, and extending the constraints in powers of these conversion variables
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
. This approach is more difficult, facing modification of local conversion scheme in view of a functional incompleteness of constraints, which probably would result in the spatially nonlocal series for the extended constraints.

We expect that the techniques developed here will provide a useful basis for further studying of GUMG-type theories. In any case, the detailed description of their dynamical and gauge properties makes them valuable as nontrivial test cases for the restricted gauge theories framework and general theory of constrained systems due to their functionally incomplete constraints, and potentially as modified gravity candidates for cosmological modeling.

Appendix A
Properties of Delocalization Operator

In this section, we use the bra-ket notation to represent the linear space of functions on compact
t{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}const
hypersurfaces of the spacetime manifold, their inner and outer products, and the linear operators acting on them. Bra- and ket-vectors denote local functions; for instance,
|f\rangle
corresponds to the local function
f({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})
, and
|1\rangle
represents the unit constant function (
f({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}){\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}1
). Inner product implies spatial integration, such that

\langle f|\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral A\pdfliteral}|g\rangle\quad\leftrightarrow\quad{\mathchoice{{\mathop{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}\textstyle{\int}}}}{{\mathop{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}\textstyle{\int}}}}{{\mathop{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{\int}}}}{{\mathop{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{\int}}}}}f\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral A\pdfliteral}\,g\,.\,

(161)

A local function appearing outside of bra- and ket- expressions denotes a diagonal operator, which acts on vector via pointwise multiplication. For example,
|W\rangle
corresponds to the local function
W({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})\defeq W\big(\!\sqrt{\gamma\hskip 0.5pt}\hskip 0.5pt\vphantom{|}({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})\big)
, while

W
outside the vector acts as a symmetric diagonal operator with the integral two-point kernel

W({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}^{\prime})\delta({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})
,
so that
W|f\rangle=|Wf\rangle\;\leftrightarrow\;W({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})f({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})
.
The
identity operator

{\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral{I}\hskip 1.0pt\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt}
corresponds to the operator with the two-point kernel
\delta({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})
and satisfies
{\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral{I}\hskip 1.0pt\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt}\hskip 1.0pt|f\rangle{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}|f\rangle
for any continuous
f({\color[rgb]{0,0.1563,0}{x}})
.
We also define symmetric projectors
{\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\hskip 1.6pt{I}\hskip 0.4pt}\pdfliteral}\hskip 2.0pt}
and
{\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral\hskip-0.5pt\hskip-1.70717pt\widetilde{\hskip 1.70717pt\hskip 1.6pt{I}\hskip 0.3pt\hskip 1.70717pt}\hskip-1.70717pt\pdfliteral}\hskip 2.1pt}{\,\hskip 1.0pt=\>}{\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral{I}\hskip 1.0pt\pdfliteral}\hskip 0.8pt}{\,-}{\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\hskip 1.6pt{I}\hskip 0.4pt}\pdfliteral}\hskip 2.0pt}
onto average and average-free components, (
23
), as follows:

{\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\hskip 1.6pt{I}\hskip 0.4pt}\pdfliteral}\hskip 2.0pt}|f\rangle\,=\,|\overline{f}\rangle\,=\,|1\rangle\overline{f}\,,\qquad{\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{\pdfliteral\hskip-0.5pt\hskip-1.70717pt\widetilde{\hskip 1.70717pt\hskip 1.6pt{I}\hskip 0.3pt\hskip 1.70717pt}\hskip-1.70717pt\pdfliteral}\hskip 2.1pt}|f\rangle\,=\,|\widetilde{f}\rangle\,=\,|f\rangle-|1\rangle\overline{f}\,.
